## At Heffreminville <note>Heffreminville refers to a location or estate in France associated with the family of the author.</note>

### Jeanne-Marie Bouvier de la Motte-Guyon <note>Madame Guyon (1648–1717) was a renowned French mystic and the leading figure of Quietism, a movement focused on finding an internal, contemplative path to God through "pure love."</note>

#### The author is Madame Guyon

<insert>M</insert> <note>Likely an abbreviation for "Madame."</note>

<image-desc>A circular bookplate label for the Library of Hermetic Philosophy <note>original: "Bibliotheca Philosophica Hermetica"</note>. The label features a light blue border with the inscription "HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY" <note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"</note> in white capital letters. Centered within the oval is a "pelican in its piety"—a classic symbol of sacrifice and Christ-like devotion, depicting a mother pelican feeding her chicks with blood from her own breast. The pelican stands atop a square pedestal decorated with a coat of arms featuring three red roses. Above the pelican, golden rays of light descend from a heavenly arc, and an Ouroboros (a serpent eating its own tail, representing eternity and the cyclical nature of the universe) forms a halo-like shape at the top of the scene.</image-desc>

<summary>This title page identifies a manuscript by the famous French mystic Madame Guyon, associated with the location Heffreminville. It contains a modern bookplate from the Ritman Library, a major collection of works on mysticism and hermetic philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Madame Guyon, Quietism, French mysticism, Heffreminville, Bibliotheca Philosophica Hermetica, pelican in its piety, Ouroboros</keywords>

# THE SONG
# OF SONGS
# OF SOLOMON,

## INTERPRETED

### ACCORDING TO THE MYSTICAL SENSE
### & the true representation of Interior
### States. <note>“Interior States” refers to the various stages of the soul's inner spiritual life and its progress toward union with God, a key concept in 17th-century French mysticism.</note>

> *All the glory of the King’s daughter is from within.* <note>original Latin: "Omnis gloria Filia Regis ab intus."</note> **Psalm 44.** <note>In the Latin Vulgate Bible used at the time, this is Psalm 44; in modern English Bibles, it is Psalm 45:13. This verse was frequently used by mystics to emphasize that true holiness is found in the heart rather than in outward ceremony.</note>

# PREFACE

WHOEVER reads this explanation of the sacred Song <note>The "Song of Songs," also known as the "Song of Solomon," a book of the Bible often interpreted as an allegory for the love between God and the soul.</note> with attention—especially if they have some discernment regarding the interior ways <note>A term in mystical theology referring to the stages of the soul's inner life and spiritual journey.</note>—will have no trouble admitting that it contains something surprising. An explanation so easy and so well-followed of one of the most obscure books of Holy Scripture can only be the fruit of a particular assistance from the Holy Spirit: <margin>* St. Bernard on the Song of Songs.</margin> since, according to the sentiments of the Saints, this Song can only be taught by Divine unction <note>A spiritual grace or "anointing" believed to provide insight into divine mysteries.</note>, nor learned except through experience: and it is not understood

<summary>This preface introduces a commentary on the Song of Songs, asserting that the book's obscurity can only be clarified through the Holy Spirit and personal spiritual experience. It draws on the authority of St. Bernard of Clairvaux to emphasize the mystical nature of the text.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Holy Spirit, St. Bernard of Clairvaux, mysticism, interior life, divine unction, biblical commentary</keywords>

# PREFACE

at all on the outside, nor does it resound in public; it is heard only by her who sings it and by him for whom it is sung, who are the Bridegroom and the Bride <note>In mystical tradition, the "Bridegroom" represents Christ (or the Divine) and the "Bride" represents the individual soul or the Church.</note>.

Every reader will find in this work features that will merit their admiration, and passages that, because they do not exceed their capacity, will be able to edify them. But only those will discover more beauties who, through the annihilation of themselves <term>Annihilation (anéantissement): A key concept in 17th-century French mysticism referring to the total surrender of the self-will to God, reaching a state of "holy nothingness."</term> and through their elevation in God, will be capable of understanding this Royal song of the Celestial Bridegroom and his Beloved <note>original: "Amante"</note>; seeing there with rapture the exact relationship between what is said here and the wonders that God works in the most purified souls. For this Song <note>original: "Cantique"</note> is only read with understanding by those who read what is sung therein, much more in the mirror of

<summary>The preface explains that the Song of Songs is an intimate, private dialogue between the soul and God. It asserts that while all readers may find edification, the text's true depths are only accessible to those who have achieved spiritual "annihilation" and union with the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Bridegroom and Bride, annihilation of self, mysticism, purified souls, spiritual edification, divine love</keywords>

-># P R E F A C E.<-

person of piety; who appears to have been chosen as another **Shulamite** <note>original: "Sulamite." In mystical tradition, the Shulamite (the bride from the Song of Songs) represents the soul in its intimate relationship with the Divine.</note>, to give us this enlightenment. There is reason to admire how she was able to declare, with such order and solidity, the secret progress of **Souls in God**, and the most unheard-of rarities of the **interior Kingdom** <note>term: interior Kingdom; the spiritual state or "space" within the heart where the soul meets God.</note>, drawing such a consistent and clear meaning from a text that appears to be without order or connection. This is all the more remarkable given that the diversity of the speakers, the frequent interruptions, and the expressions—surprising in their detachment and presented under a continuous allegory—seemingly offered nothing from which one could derive, with such precision, the explanation of the beginning, the progress, and the consummation of the **interior way** <note>term: interior way; the contemplative path of spiritual growth and purification.</note>.

<margin>Denis the Carthusian <note>original: "Denis le Chartr." Refers to Denis the Carthusian (1402–1471), a famous theologian and mystic known as the "Ecstatic Doctor."</note></margin> An infinite number of works have been written to interpret this entirely Divine Book:

<summary>This page credits a "person of piety" (the author) with providing a clear, orderly map of the soul's journey within the "interior Kingdom," despite the inherent complexity and allegorical nature of the Song of Songs. It positions this work among the long history of interpretations of this biblical text.</summary>
<keywords>Shulamite, interior Kingdom, interior way, allegory, Song of Songs, Denis the Carthusian, mystical progress</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the work, further describing the unique nature of the author's inspiration and the spiritual origins of the text.</meta>

-># P R E F A C E.<-

Some works are the result of study, others are the fruit of **Prayer** <note>original: "Oraiſon." In mystical tradition, this refers to contemplative or interior prayer—a silent communion with God—rather than just spoken petitions.</note>, and others have been dictated by the overflowing fullness caused by **Divine union**. But this one will be distinguished as entirely new in its kind, even though its truth is eternal in God. It will be observed that it is so singular that it may pass for an original in this matter, especially since it was produced without premeditation and without any book other than the **Sacred Text** <note>original: "ſacré Texte." This refers to the Bible, specifically the Song of Songs, which the author is interpreting.</note>.

May the humble and pious Reader admire the profusion of Divine goodness toward the **Souls** who are faithful to Him, attributing nothing to the **Creature** <note>term: Creature; a common spiritual term for a human being, used to emphasize our status as created beings who are nothing without the Creator.</note> but the wretchedness that is natural to it; and may he glorify the Lord for everything solid and edifying found in this work.

Solomon, by a certain movement—

<summary>The preface explains that this commentary was produced through divine inspiration and direct engagement with the Bible rather than academic study. It encourages readers to credit God’s goodness for the work’s insights rather than the human author.</summary>
<keywords>Divine union, Prayer, Sacred Text, humility, Solomon, spiritual inspiration, the Creature</keywords>

This refers to the biblical Song of Solomon (or Song of Songs), which is traditionally interpreted as an allegory of divine love.</note> of the chaste loves, the secret communications, the mutual fidelity, the intimate union, and the sacred Marriage of **JESUS CHRIST** with His Church.

But this even extends to every pure **Soul**, as being an illustrious member of this **mystical Body** <note>term: mystical Body; a theological concept describing the spiritual union of all Christian believers into a single entity with Christ as the "head."</note>, of which He is the head. In a word, there is included here the summary of all that the **Savior** has done for the Church, His principal Bride; and also what He has done <margin>St. Bonaventure, in the *Mirror of Novices*, Part 1, Chapter 12.</margin> for each soul in particular. This adorable Bridegroom having done for each of His beloved souls what He has done for all in general.

Light the most vivid flames:

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page follows the dedication to the Infant Jesus. It transitions from the author's personal devotion to the legal and official authorization required for publication in 17th-century France. This document, the "King's Privilege," establishes the copyright and legal protections for the work.</meta>

### Extract of the King’s Privilege.

By grace and Privilege of the King, given at Versailles on the 10th day of July, 1687. Signed by the King in his Council, De-Lamet, and sealed with the Great Seal of Yellow Wax, permission is granted to **Antoine Briasson**, Bookseller <note>original: "Libraire" — In this period, a "libraire" often acted as both a bookseller and a publisher.</note> at Lyon, to have printed a Book entitled, *the Song of Songs of Solomon, interpreted according to the mystic sense, and the true representation of interior states*, and this for the time and space of six consecutive years, counting from the day it shall be finished printing for the first time; with prohibitions to all persons of whatever quality and condition they may be, from printing it or having it printed, without the consent of the said Briasson or his successors; under penalty of two thousand livres <note>The "livre" was the currency of France until 1795. Two thousand livres was a very substantial sum, intended to deter literary piracy.</note> fine, confiscation of the counterfeit copies, and of all costs, damages, and interests, as is contained more at length in the said letters of privilege.

->Registered in the Book of the Guild of Booksellers & Printers of Paris, the 21st of July 1687. Signed,<-

->**J. B. Coignard**, Syndic <term>Syndic: An elected official of a professional guild—in this case, the book trade—responsible for legal administration and enforcing regulations.</term>.<-

---

Finished Printing for the first time on the 15th of September 1687.

->The copies have been provided <note>original: "Les Exemplaires ont été fournis" — This refers to the legal deposit (dépôt légal), where the publisher was required to provide a certain number of copies to the royal libraries to maintain the privilege.</note>.<-

<summary>This page contains the official "King's Privilege," a legal document granting Antoine Briasson the exclusive rights to publish the book for six years. It details the legal protections against piracy and records the official registration with the Paris guild of printers.</summary>
<keywords>King's Privilege, Antoine Briasson, Song of Songs, publishing history, copyright, Lyon, Paris, 1687, legal deposit</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page follows the "Extract of the King's Privilege," transitioning from the legal right to publish (the secular authority) to the formal ecclesiastical approvals (the religious authority). These "approbations" were essential in 17th-century France to certify that the work's mystical interpretations were doctrinally sound and divinely inspired.</meta>

# APPROVALS.

The Book of the Song of Songs <note>Also known as the Canticle of Canticles, this book of the Bible is traditionally read as a dialogue of love between God and the soul.</note> is so mysterious, that it belongs only to those who have the spirit, the capacity, and the piety of St. Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153) wrote a famous series of 86 sermons on the Song of Songs; he was considered the ultimate authority on its mystical meaning.</note> to penetrate and explain it, and history testifies that even people highly accomplished in doctrine have not dared to undertake the unfolding of these hidden meanings and these sacred enigmas; which is why I am seized with astonishment when I consider that the Author of this Book has explained the Song in a manner so easy, so beautiful, and so edifying. The author is indebted for this to the communications of the Holy Spirit, which blows where it wills; I offer this testimony in their favor, at Lyon, August 20, the day and feast of St. Bernard, in the year 1687.

->COHADE.<-

---

THE undersigned Priest, Doctor in Canon Law, Bachelor of the Sorbonne <note>The prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris.</note>, General Syndic of the Clergy of Lyon, Custodian of the Parish of the Holy Cross, and Lieutenant in the Ordinary and Metropolitan Officiality <term>Officiality: The ecclesiastical court of a diocese, responsible for judicial and administrative matters under the Bishop.</term> of this Diocese, Attests that this explanation of the Book of the Song of Songs is all the more to be esteemed because the subject matter is very delicate to treat. These are certain mysteries of the kingdom of God, which it is not given to everyone to know; most see them only under very dark shadows, and read them only as parables whose meaning is hidden from the proud sages of the age, and revealed only to the disciples of the spirit which rests upon the humble. Blessed then is the Author of this Book who has so wisely, so truly, and so loftily known and explained this most mysterious sense, and blessed will be those who read this explanation in this same spirit of faith, of religion, and of virtue, and with a very deep and constant humility; this is my sentiment. At Lyon, this 5th of September 1687.

->TERRASSON.<-

<summary>This page contains two formal ecclesiastical approvals from religious officials in Lyon, certifying that the author's interpretation of the Song of Songs is divinely inspired and doctrinally sound. The endorsers emphasize the difficulty of the text and the humility required to understand its mystical secrets.</summary>
<keywords>approvals, Song of Songs, St. Bernard, Lyon, mysticism, Sorbonne, Holy Spirit, ecclesiastical authority, humility</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical distinction between different types of spiritual union, clarifying that the soul remains distinct from God in essence even when transformed in experience. ### [The Song] of Solomon.

...and not according to the real stripping away of the intimate subsistence, which is necessary for the <term>hypostatic union: the theological doctrine of the union of divine and human natures in the single person of Jesus Christ</term>. Rather, it is like a drop of water that loses its perceptible consistency when it is placed into a vat of wine, where it is perceptibly changed into wine; although its being and its matter always remain distinct from it: and an Angel could, if God willed it, perform the division. In the same way, this soul can always be separated from its God, although such a thing would be very difficult.

It is, therefore, this high and intimate union that the <term>Bride: in mystical tradition, the human soul seeking God</term> asks of her <term>Groom: representing God or Christ</term> with such persistence. She asks it of him as if speaking to another person: it is an impetuous burst of her love, which, without considering to whom she speaks, gives flight to her passion. *Let him kiss me*, she says, since he is able to do so, *but with the kiss of his mouth*: <note>original: "Qu'il me baise... du baiser de sa bouche." This refers to the opening of the Song of Songs 1:2.</note> any other union cannot satisfy me at all; that one alone can satisfy all my desires, and it is that which I ask for.

### The Song of Songs

<margin>
VERSES 1, 2.

For your breasts are better than wine, fragrant with the best ointments.
<note>original Latin: "Quia meliora sunt ubera tua vino, fragrantia unguentis optimis."</note>
</margin>

> *For your breasts are better than wine: and more fragrant than excellent perfumes.*

The <term>breasts: in this mystical tradition, a metaphor for the initial graces and comforts God provides to those beginning their spiritual journey</term>, O God, with which you nourish beginning souls, are so sweet and so pleasant that they make your children—even your children who still have need of the breast—stronger than the most robust men who drink wine. They are so fragrant that, by their charming scent, they attract the souls who have the happiness of smelling them; they are also like a precious balm, which heals all internal wounds. If it is already like this in these first approaches, how much delight will there be in the nuptial kiss, the kiss of the mouth?

At the beginning of this Song, that which must be its end is proposed, acting as the reward and the perfect consummation of the <term>Bride: the soul in its most intimate relationship with the Divine</term>. This is because it is natural that the vision and the desire for the goal precede the choice of the means. Subsequently, the means to arrive there are described in order.

<meta>catchword: means</meta>

<summary>This page interprets the biblical metaphor of the "breasts" as the initial, healing grace God gives to new believers, which is sweeter than wine. It also explains that the Song of Songs presents the ultimate goal of union at the very beginning to inspire the soul to follow the necessary path to reach it.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, divine grace, spiritual nourishment, the Bride, mystical union, perfumes, healing balm</keywords>

...starting with spiritual infancy. ### The Song of Songs

<margin>
**VERSE 4.**

I am black but beautiful, O daughters of Jerusalem: like the tents of Kedar, like the curtains of Solomon. <note>original Latin: "Nigra sum sed formosa filiæ Ierusalem : sicut tabernacula Cedar, sicut pelles Salomonis."</note>
</margin>

->*O daughters of Jerusalem! I am black, but beautiful, like the tents of Kedar, like the curtains of Solomon.*<-

Since the greatest <term>graces: divine gifts or favors from God</term> always tend toward a deeper knowledge of what we are, and since they would not be from him if they did not provide, according to their degree, a certain experience of the <term>creature's misery: the inherent weakness and unworthiness of the human being when compared to the divine</term>, this <term>soul: the Bride</term> has barely left the <term>cellars: representing the secret, intimate places of divine experience mentioned in the previous verse</term> of her <term>Groom: Christ</term> when she finds herself "black." 

What is your blackness, O incomparable <term>Lover: the soul in love with God</term>? Tell us: we implore you. "I am black," she says, "because by the light of my divine <term>Sun: God, whose light reveals the hidden truth of the soul</term>, I perceive a multitude of defects that I had ignored until now. I am black because I am not at all purified of my <term>self-interest: the mystical concept of 'propriété' or 'self-hood'—the attachment to one's own ego and will</term>." 

But nevertheless, I do not cease to be beautiful, and beautiful like the <term>tents of Kedar: Kedar refers to nomadic tribes whose tents were made of black goat hair; they were weathered and dark on the outside but often contained great riches within</term>. This is because this experiential knowledge of what I am is extremely pleasing to my Groom and invites him to come into me, as into a place of rest. I am beautiful because, having no
<meta>catchword: point / [at all]</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>of Solomon.</header>

...no voluntary stain, my <term>Groom: Christ, the spiritual husband of the soul</term> makes me beautiful with his own beauty. The more I am black in my own eyes, the more I am beautiful in him.

I am also beautiful, like the <term>pavilions of Solomon: referring to the ornate tents or hangings of the King, which symbolize a royal and divine dwelling</term>. The pavilions of the divine Solomon are the <term>Holy Humanity: the human nature of Jesus Christ</term>, which covers within itself the <term>Word of God: the eternal Son or Logos</term> made flesh. I am beautiful, she says, like his pavilions; because he has made me a participant in his beauty, in that just as his Holy Humanity covers his Divinity, so too my apparent blackness hides the greatness of God's operations in my <term>soul: the inner life where God works</term>.

I am also black through the <term>crosses: the burdens, trials, and sufferings of life</term> and the persecutions that come to me from the outside. But I am beautiful like the pavilions of Solomon, since these crosses and this blackness make me like him. I am black because weaknesses appear in my outward behavior; but I am beautiful because I am inwardly free from <term>malice: intentional evil or sinful attachment</term>.

<sig>B</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical fleuron with symmetrical leaf and scroll motifs.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the mystical paradox of the soul being "black but beautiful" by comparing it to Christ’s humanity, which veiled his divinity. It argues that outward suffering and perceived weaknesses (blackness) actually hide and protect a profound inner beauty and likeness to God.</summary>

### VERSE 5.

<margin>Do not look at me because I am dark; because the sun has discolored me: the sons of my mother have fought against me, they have placed me as a keeper in the vineyards: my own vineyard I have not kept. <note>original Latin: "Nolite me considerare quòd fusca sim; quia decoloravit me sol: filii matris meæ pugnaverunt contra me, posuerunt me custodem in vineis: vineam meam non custodivi."</note></margin>

> *Do not gaze upon me because I am brown; because it is the sun that has discolored me. My mother’s children have fought against me: they have set me to guard the vineyards. I have not kept my own vineyard.*

Why does the <term>Bride: the soul in its mystical union with God</term> ask that one does not look at her in her blackness? It is because the <term>soul: the inner spiritual life</term>, beginning to enter the state of <term>faith: a stage of spiritual growth where one relies on trust rather than emotional or sensory "proofs"</term> and the stripping away of <term>sensible graces: the pleasant emotional feelings and "sweetness" often felt by beginners in prayer</term>, gradually loses that sweet vigor which allowed her to practice good works with ease, and which made her appear entirely beautiful to those on the outside. And being no longer able to fulfill her former practices because God requires something else of her, it seems as though she has fallen back into her natural state. It appears this way to those who are not enlightened. This is why she says: I entreat you, my companions, who have not yet arrived so far into the <term>interior: the deep, inward life of the spirit</term>, you who are only in the pre-

<meta>The text ends mid-word "pre-", likely "premiers" (first) or "preliminaires" (preliminaries), indicating continuation on the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that as a soul matures, it loses the "sweet" outward signs of devotion (sensible graces) and enters a "darker" state of pure faith, which onlookers may mistake for a spiritual decline. This "discoloration" is actually the result of God's direct action, like the sun darkening the skin.</summary>

[first] steps of the <term>spiritual life: the journey of the soul toward union with God</term>; do not judge me by the brown color that I wear on the outside; nor by all my external faults, whether real or apparent; for this does not happen as it does with <term>beginning souls: those in the early stages of faith who still rely on emotional "sweetness" in prayer</term>, for lack of love and courage: but it is because my divine Sun, through his continual, ardent, and burning gazes, has discolored me. *I am dark; because it is the sun that has discolored me. My mother’s children have fought against me: they have established me to guard the vineyards. I have not guarded my own vineyard.*

Why does the <term>Bride: the soul in pursuit of divine union</term> ask that she not be looked at in her darkness? It is because as the soul begins to enter into the <term>state of faith: a stage where the soul relies on pure trust rather than emotional experiences</term>, and into the stripping away of <term>sensible graces: the "sweet" or pleasant feelings often experienced by beginners in prayer</term>, she gradually loses that sweet vigor which once allowed her to practice virtue with ease, and which made her appear beautiful to those outside. And being no longer able to perform her former spiritual practices, because God desires something else from her, it seems as though she has fallen back into her natural state. This is how it appears to those who are not spiritually enlightened. This is why she says: I implore you, my companions—you who have not yet reached so far into the <term>interior life: the deep, internal journey of the soul</term>, you who are only in the first... <note>The text here breaks off and continues on page 19 with "steps of the spiritual life."</note>

<summary>The author explains that the soul's loss of outward spiritual "beauty" and emotional "sweetness" is a necessary stage of growth, often misunderstood by observers as a spiritual decline.</summary>

# of Solomon.

...steps of the spiritual life; do not judge me by the dark color I wear on the outside, nor by all my outward flaws, whether real or apparent; for this does not happen as it does to <term>beginning souls: those in the earliest stages of spiritual practice who might fail through lack of discipline</term>, for lack of love and courage. Instead, it is because my divine Sun, through his constant, ardent, and burning gazes, has discolored me. He has taken away my natural color, to leave me only with that which his ardor wishes to give me. It is the strength of love that dries my skin and browns it, and not the absence of love. 

This darkness is an advancement, and not a flaw. However, it is a progress that you must not consider, you who are still young and too tender to imitate it; because the darkness you would give yourselves <note>The author warns against "self-made" suffering or artificial humility, which would be a spiritual defect.</note> would be a flaw. To be good, this darkness must only come from the <term>Sun of Justice: a traditional title for Christ, representing his purifying light</term>, who for his glory and for the soul's greatest good, eats and devours that brilliant outward color which blinded the soul itself—even though it made her admirable to others, to the detriment of the glory of the <term>Spouse: the Divine Bridegroom, or Christ</term>.

# The Song of Songs

->B<-

My brothers, seeing me dark in this manner, wished to force me to return to the <term>active life: a way of living focused on external works, service, and duties, often contrasted with the "contemplative life" of prayer</term>, and to tend to outward appearances, without applying myself to putting to death the <term>passions: in this context, the internal emotional and spiritual impulses that distract the soul from God</term> of the interior. I struggled with them for a long time; but finally, being unable to resist them, I did what they wanted: and by applying myself to the outside, to things that are foreign to me <note>The author suggests that for a soul called to deep contemplation, purely external duties can feel like an alienation from their true purpose.</note>, I have not kept my <term>vineyard: a metaphor for the soul's own interior life and its relationship with God</term>, which is my very depth, where my God dwells. 

This is my only concern, and the only vineyard I ought to keep: and when I did not keep my own—when I did not remain attentive to my God—I kept the others even less. This is the torment usually inflicted upon souls when people see that a great preoccupation with the <term>interior: the "inner man" or the private space of the heart where the soul meets the Divine</term> causes them to neglect <term>outward things: social conventions, external religious rituals, or worldly business</term> in some way. Because of this, the soul, being entirely enclosed within, can no longer apply herself to certain small flaws, which the <term>Spouse: Christ, the Bridegroom of the soul</term> will correct at another time.

# The Song of Songs

Therefore, let the <term>creature: the individual human soul as a created being</term> who aspires to divine union—being well-convinced of the "All" of God and of its own <term>nothingness: "le néant," a central concept in French mysticism referring to the soul's total lack of inherent power or goodness apart from God</term>—go out from itself, <note>The original French transcription "forte" is likely a misreading of "sorte" (to go out), which aligns with the subsequent sentence and the overarching theme of the "sortie" or exit from the self.</note> having only contempt and hatred for itself, so as to reserve all its esteem and love for God; and by this very path, she shall be admitted to His union. This "going out" from oneself, through the continual renunciation of all self-interest, is the interior exercise that the <term>Heavenly Lover: God or Christ as the bridegroom of the soul</term> counsels to those Souls who sigh for the <term>kiss of the mouth: a metaphor from Song of Songs 1:2 representing the highest point of mystical union</term>. As He gives her to understand by this single word, "*go out*," which is sufficient for her to regulate her interior life.

But as for the exterior, He wishes that she neglect nothing of all that is her duty, in the <term>state: "l'état," the specific social or religious station in life (such as marriage or the convent) ordained by God</term> in which He has placed her; this encompasses infinitely more than all the details that one could possibly list. He wishes, furthermore, that just as she must follow the attraction of the Holy Spirit with all liberty in everything regarding her interior life, she must also conform to the customs of the Church and to the orders of her <term>Superiors: those in positions of religious authority, such as confessors or church leaders</term> in everything regarding her exterior life. This is well-signified by the phrase *walking in the footsteps of the*

It emphasizes that while the soul's interior life is guided by the Holy Spirit, its exterior life must remain obedient to church duties and authority.</summary>

<margin>
VERSE 9.
</margin>
<margin>
<note>original Latin: "Pulchræ sunt genæ tuæ sicut turturis: Collum sicut monilia."</note>
Your cheeks are beautiful like those of a turtledove: your neck is like necklaces.
</margin>
*Your cheeks are beautiful, like those of a turtledove: your neck resembles pearls.*

These cheeks signify the interior and the exterior: they are beautiful like those of the turtledove. The turtledove has this specific trait: when one of the pair dies, the one that survives remains alone for the rest of its days, without mating with another. <note>In medieval and early modern natural history, the turtledove was a symbol of perpetual chastity and faithful grieving for a lost spouse.</note> In the same way, the soul that finds itself far from its God cannot take pleasure in any creature, neither outwardly, nor in the...

<summary>The Bridegroom explains that the soul's rapid spiritual progress may look like worldly chaos to outsiders, then uses the turtledove to symbolize the soul's exclusive devotion to God.</summary>

These ornaments will be necklaces, as a sign of your perfect submission to all the desires of the <term>King of Glory: a traditional title for God or Christ, emphasizing His majestic and divine nature</term>: but they will be made of gold, to represent that, acting only through a most refined love, you have only the simple and pure aim of the good pleasure and the glory of God in everything you do or suffer for Him.

To mark, nonetheless, the progress of this **Soul**, already heroic, she does not say "my **Beloved** will give me the bouquet of the **Cross**"; but he himself will be this bouquet, for all my **Crosses** will be those of my **Beloved**: the bouquet will be between my breasts, as a sign that he must be to me a <term>Spouse of bitterness: a reference to the "Bridegroom of Blood" in Exodus 4:25, here signifying a soul joined to Christ through suffering</term>, as much for the outside as for the inside.

Exterior **Crosses** do very little when they are not accompanied by interior ones; and the interior ones are made much more painful by the union of the exterior. But although the soul perceives only the cross on every side, it is nevertheless her **Beloved**, who is himself this cross; and he was never more present to her than in these bitternesses, during which he remains in the middle of her heart.

VERSE 15.

### How beautiful you are, my Beloved, how pleasant you are! Our bed is adorned with flowers.

The loving soul <term>loving soul: "l'Ame amante," a common term in French mysticism for the individual spirit seeking union with God</term> seeing that her Spouse has praised her for a double beauty, and not wishing to attribute anything to herself, says to him also: *How beautiful you are, my Beloved; and how pleasant you are!* She returns to him all the praises she receives from him, and she gives him very great ones in her turn. Since no good belongs to us, no praise, no glory, no pleasure should stop with us: everything must be sent back to him, who is the author and the center of all good. The Bride in all this discourse teaches us this important practice, glorifying the Lord in all things for everything he has placed within her. If I am beautiful, she says to him, it is from your own beauty; it is you who are beautiful in me with this double beauty for which you praise me. Our bed, she adds—this depth <note>original: "fond," referring to the "ground of the soul" or the innermost spiritual center</note> where you dwell in me, which I call "ours" to invite you to come and give me this nuptial kiss <note>original: "baiser nuptial"</note>—

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Verse 1 of Chapter 2, where the Bridegroom (Christ) identifies Himself as the "flower of the field" and "lily of the valleys." It then transitions into Verse 2, contrasting the "annihilated" soul with those who remain attached to their own will.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>The Song of Songs</header>

of combat, of labor, and of suffering. I am the lily of the valleys, which only grows in annihilated souls <note>The term "annihilated souls" (ames aneanties) refers to a central concept in 17th-century French mysticism, where the soul reaches a state of total humility and "nothingness" before God, stripping away the ego.</note>. Thus, if you want me to pull you from your earth, and for me to take life within you, you must be in the ultimate state of annihilation: and if you wish to find me, you must enter into combat and into suffering.

<margin>VERSE 2.
As the lily among thorns, so is my love among the daughters. <note>original Latin: "Sicut lilium inter spinas, sic amica mea inter filias."</note></margin>

->***My Beloved is among the daughters like the lily among the thorns.***<-

The Bridegroom, through these words, makes known the progress of his Beloved <term>Amante: the soul portrayed as the female lover of Christ</term>, who is like a most pure, most agreeable lily of sweet fragrance before him; while the other daughters, instead of being supple and pliable, and allowing themselves to be led by his spirit, are like thorn bushes, which bristle and prick those who wish to approach them. Such are the self-possessing souls <term>ames proprietaires: a technical term for souls who are still attached to their own ego, merits, or "ownership" of their spiritual progress</term> and attached to their own will, who do not want to let themselves be led to God. And this is what a soul truly abandoned to her God...

The King's beloved, emerging from the sweet conversation she has just had with him, appears to her companions as if drunk and entirely beside herself. She was indeed so; for having drunk the most excellent wine of the Groom <note>The "Groom" or "Bridegroom" is a traditional allegorical representation of Christ in his relationship with the soul or the Church.</note>, she could not help but be inflamed with the strongest ardor. She was affected in such a way that, perceiving it quite clearly herself, she begs her companions not to be surprised to see her in such an extraordinary state. "My intoxication," she tells them, "is entirely pardonable; since my King has made me enter into these divine cellars." The daughters of Jerusalem <note>In mystical tradition, the "daughters of Jerusalem" often represent less-advanced souls or external spiritual activities that can distract a contemplative from deep union with God.</note> act as those charitable but troublesome friends who strive so hard to pull her away from there, albeit under the finest pretexts; but she is so deep in sleep that she cannot emerge from it. The Bridegroom <note>original: "l’Epoux." "Do not wake her," he tells them, "neither directly nor indirectly, by using any means sought out for that purpose, until she herself is willing; because she will only want it when I want it."

# The Song of Songs

### VERSE 8.

<margin>The voice of my beloved, behold he comes leaping on the mountains, skipping over the hills. <note>original Latin: "Vox dilecti mei, ecce iste venit saliens in montibus, transiliens colles."</note></margin>

*Behold the voice of my Beloved; here he comes leaping upon the mountains, and passing over the hills.*

This Soul, which is asleep to everything else, is more attentive to the voice of her Beloved: she hears him and distinguishes him at once: *Behold the voice of my Beloved*, she says: I know him, I hear him, and the effect he works within me does not allow me to doubt it. But what are you saying, O Lover? Love perhaps makes you dream: you sleep in the arms of your Beloved, and yet you say that he comes even upon the mountains, and that he passes over the hills! Oh, how well all this accords! The Bridegroom embraces his Lover, and he is in her: He surrounds her from without, and he penetrates her within: she feels that in this mystical sleep <term>mystical sleep: a state of deep contemplative prayer where the soul's external senses are quieted, allowing for a profound, direct experience of the divine presence.</term>, he sinks into her, that he unites with her, not only as before, through the faculties <note>The "faculties" refer to the powers of the mind, such as memory, understanding, and will.</note>, which constitute the hills: but that moreover, passing over the hills,

This movement signifies God penetrating deeper into the soul, moving beyond the mental faculties (the hills) to the very center of her being.</summary>

I hear my Beloved who says to me: Arise, make haste my Beloved, my dove, my beautiful one, and come.

God, having entirely turned the Soul inward upon itself, and having led it to its center after having made it enjoy his chaste embraces to prepare it for the spiritual marriage <term>spiritual marriage: the highest state of mystical union where the soul is permanently united with God</term>; he now makes it take a path that is entirely opposite in appearance: he makes it go out of itself through mystical death <term>mystical death: in French, "trepas mistique," a spiritual state where the soul's own will and ego "die" so it can be moved solely by God</term>. The Beloved, coming himself to speak to this Soul, invites it to depart promptly: He no longer tells it to rest; on the contrary, he commands it to rise from its rest. This is a very different manner from that which he previously held: He used to forbid anyone from waking her <note>A reference to Verse 7: "Do not stir up nor awaken love until it pleases."</note>, and now he wants her to rise promptly. He calls her in a manner so sweet and so strong that even if she were not as passionate to obey him as she is, she would be unable to resist.

# The Song of Songs

*you, my Beloved, whom I have chosen to make my Spouse, and my all-beautiful one; for I find you beautiful in my eyes, noticing in you a thousand features of my beauty. My dove, simple and faithful; arise and come out, since you have all the necessary qualities to go out from yourself. Having drawn you within yourself, I now step out, as it were, from you, to oblige you to come out by following me.*

<margin>* Chap. 1. verse 7. <note>The author likely refers back to the earlier discussion of Verse 7, where the soul was commanded to rest, contrasting it with this new command to "arise."</note></margin>
This departure <term>departure: French "sortie," a mystical term for the soul leaving behind its own self-interest and ego to be absorbed in God</term> is very different from the one spoken of * above, and much more advanced: for the first was a departure from natural satisfactions, in order to desire to please only the Beloved, but this one is a departure from the possession of oneself, so as to no longer be possessed by anything but God; and so that, no longer perceiving herself within herself, she finds herself only in him. It is a transport of the creature into its origin, as will be declared in what follows.

<summary>The author distinguishes between two types of spiritual "departure": an earlier stage where one gives up worldly pleasures, and this more advanced stage where the soul abandons its very self to be fully possessed by God.</summary>

of Solomon. VERSE 11. <note>The original text reads "VERS. II.", likely a printing error for XI (11), as the text corresponds to Song of Songs 2:11.</note>

## for lo, the winter is past; the rain is over and gone.

<margin>
For now the
winter has 
passed, the 
rain has departed, 
and receded. 
<note>original Latin: "Iam enim hiems transiit, imber abiit, & recessit."</note>
</margin>

One must know that there are two winters: the one without <note>meaning external or outward</note>, and the one within <note>meaning internal or inward</note>; and that both are mutually contrary to each other. When it is winter on the outside, it is summer on the inside, which leads the soul to sink deeper into itself by an effect of grace that produces a profound <term>recollection: French "recueillement," a state of drawing all one's faculties inward to focus on the presence of God</term>. And when it is winter on the inside, a summer is created on the outside, which obliges the Soul to go out of itself through the expansion caused by a more extensive grace of <term>abandonment: French "abandon," the mystical practice of total surrender to the divine will</term>. 

The winter of which the Bridegroom speaks here, saying that it is already past, is the external winter, during which the Soul could be frozen by the rigor of the cold, soiled by the rains, and overwhelmed under the storms and under the snows of sins and imperfections that one easily contracts in the <term>commerce of creatures: interaction with the worldly things and people instead of focusing on the Creator</term>. The Soul that has found the center has been so fortified that there is nothing more for her to fear from the outside: all the rains are wiped away; and it

This spiritual maturity allows the soul to safely follow the Bridegroom's call to come out of itself.</summary>

it would be impossible for her, unless by the darkest infidelity <note>In mystical theology, "infidelity" refers to the soul turning its will away from God toward worldly attachments.</note> ever known, to take any pleasure in outward things. Furthermore, this way of speaking—*the winter is already past*—means that, just as winter deadens all things, so for this Soul, death has passed over all external things; consequently, there is nothing left that can satisfy her. If anything still appears there, it is a renewal of innocence, which no longer possesses any of the malice of former times. The winter rains have also passed; she can go out without fearing the winter anymore, and with this advantage: that the winter has destroyed and put to death what was formerly alive for her, and which would have caused her own death; just as the rigor of winter purges the earth of vermin <note>original: "infectes," here referring to harmful insects or pests that the cold kills off</note>.

### VERSE 12.

*The flowers begin to appear on our land, the time for pruning the vine has come; the voice of the turtle-dove has been heard in our country.*

In order to persuade her to come, he lets her understand that he wishes to lead her into his land: He calls it *our land*, because he has acquired it for her through his redemption, and because it belongs to him for her sake, and to her through him. He says that the flowers have already begun to appear in that place; but these are flowers that never wither: flowers that no longer fear the approach of winter. *The time*, he says, *to prune the vine has come*. ### Verse 13.

<margin>
The fig tree has brought forth its early figs; the blossoming vines have given their fragrance. Arise, my beloved, my beautiful one, and come. <note>original Latin: "Ficus protulit grossos suos, vineæ florentes dederunt odorem suum. Surge amica mea, speciosa mea, & veni."</note>
</margin>
*The fig tree has produced its early figs <note>The French text uses "fausses figues" (false figs), referring to the green, unripe fruit that appears on the tree before the leaves and the main harvest.</note>, the blossoming vines spread their pleasant fragrance. Arise, my Beloved, my beautiful one, and come.*

There, springtime is eternal, and it blends perfectly with the fruits of autumn and the heat of summer. The Bridegroom, through these flowers and fruits, indicates three seasons clearly enough; but he does not speak of winter at all. This is because, as has been said, when the Soul arrives in this new land, she finds that winter—not only the external but even the internal <term>winter</term> <note>In mystical theology, "internal winter" refers to a period of spiritual coldness, dryness, or the "dark night" where the soul feels abandoned by God to purge it of self-love.</note>—has passed. There is no longer any winter for a Soul who has arrived in God; instead, there is a composite of the three other seasons, which find themselves all reunited into one, and as if immortalized by the loss of winter. For before reaching the internal winter, the Soul has passed through all the seasons of the spiritual life; but after the internal win-

He gives us all the means for this: He draws us by his pressing inspirations; why do we not run?

O inestimable happiness of a Soul, who belongs entirely and without reserve to her Beloved, and to whom the Beloved is all things! The Lover <term>Lover</term> <note>French: "Amante." She says that her Beloved belongs to her, to dispose of as He pleases; and that she is also entirely His, for all His will: that He takes His rest in her, among the lilies of her purity. # of Solomon. VERSE 2.

<margin>I will rise and go about the city, through the streets and the squares; I will seek him whom my soul loves; I sought him, and I found him not. <note>original Latin: "Surgam & circuibo civitatem, per vicos & plateas, quæram quem diligit anima mea; quæsivi illum, & non inveni."</note></margin>

*I must rise and go about the city: I will seek through the streets and the public squares the one my soul loves: I sought him, and I found him not.*

O miracle worked by the absence of a God! How many times had he invited his Lover to rise from her rest, and she could not yet do it? He pressed her with the most tender words in the world: Nevertheless, she was so intoxicated with the peace and tranquility she was tasting, that she could not leave it. O faithful soul <term>Ame fidelle</term> <note>This refers to the soul committed to the path of "interior prayer," a movement in 17th-century spirituality that emphasized finding God within.</note>, the rest you enjoy within yourself is but a shadow of rest compared to that which you will find in God. It was nevertheless impossible to make her rise; but as soon as she no longer finds her Beloved <term>Bien-aimé</term> <note>The "Beloved" or "Spouse" represents Christ in this mystical interpretation of the Song of Songs.</note> in the bed of her rest; "Oh!" she says, "how well I will rise now! This bed of rest, which was once a paradise to me, is now a hell, since my Beloved is absent."

# The Song of Songs

...and with him, hell would be a paradise to me. This city, this world, which I once hated, will henceforth be the place of my search. For this soul, not yet fully instructed—however passionate she may seem, and with good reason, for the possession of her sovereign good and her final end—here displays the feelings of a child. She is so weak that it is impossible for her to seek God in himself at first, even though she no longer finds him in her depths <note>French: "son fond"—the "ground" or innermost center of the soul where mystical union occurs.</note>; she seeks him in all created things, in a thousand places where he is not; and being thus scattered outside herself, she trifles with the creature <term>la creature</term> <note>In 17th-century spiritual writing, "the creature" refers to any created thing—whether a person, an object, or a feeling—as distinct from the Creator.</note> under the pretext of seeking the Creator. She seeks, nonetheless, for her heart loves; and it can find no rest except in what it loves. But she finds nothing, because God has not left her to make himself sought among other creatures. He wishes to be sought in himself; and when she has arrived in him, she will discover there another truth whose beauty will ravish her: which is that her Beloved is everywhere and in everything, and that everything is he himself, without her being able to distinguish anything from him, who is

<summary>The author explains that the soul initially makes the mistake of seeking God in external, created things rather than within itself. This "childish" stage of spirituality is necessary but ultimately unsuccessful until the soul learns that God must be found in His own essence.</summary>

<keywords>Song of Solomon, the Soul, the Beloved, interiority, created things, mystical search, divine presence, the Creator</keywords>

->VERSE 3.<-

<margin>The watchmen who guard the city found me: Have you seen him whom my soul loves? <note>original Latin: "Invenerunt me vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem: Nam quem diligit anima mea, vidistis."</note></margin>
*The watchmen who guard the city found me; have you not seen him whom my soul loves?*

<margin>Isaiah 62, v. 2. <note>While the text cites verse 2, the famous reference to watchmen on the walls of Jerusalem is found in Isaiah 62:6.</note></margin>
<margin>John 20, v. 12, etc.</margin>
As I saw that I could not find my Beloved in any mortal creature, I sought him among the blessed spirits who guard the city; they found me, because they are always watching. These are the guards whom God has established upon the walls of Jerusalem, who shall never hold their peace, neither by day nor by night. I therefore asked them for news of my Beloved, of him for whom I burn with ardor; but although they possess him for themselves, they could not give him to me. It seems to me that I see Mary Magdalene, who, not finding Jesus Christ in the tomb, seeks him everywhere, asking the Angels and men for him; but no one can give an account of the Beloved except himself.

<summary>The soul seeks the Beloved among the "watchmen" (angels and saints), symbolizing a stage of spiritual growth where one looks to heavenly intermediaries for guidance, only to find that only the Beloved can truly reveal himself.</summary>

### VERSE 4.

<margin>
When I had passed by them a little, I found him whom my soul loves: I held him, and I will not let him go: until I bring him into my mother's house, and into the chamber of her who bore me. <note>original Latin: "Paululum cùm pertransiſſem eos inveni quē diligit anima mea: tenui eū, nec dimittā : donec introducā illum in domū matris meæ, & in cubiculum genitricis meæ."</note>
</margin>

*Scarcely had I passed them when I found him whom my soul loves; I held him and I will not let him go, until I have brought him into my mother’s house, and into the chamber of her who bore me.*

The Soul, having left herself behind and having surpassed all created things, encounters her Beloved, who shows himself to her with new charms. This persuades her that the fortunate moment of the consummation of the marriage is near, and that the permanent union is about to be forged. In the rapture <note>French: "transport," referring to a state of spiritual ecstasy</note> in which she finds herself because of the happiness she possesses, she cries out: I have my Beloved, I have found him, I hold him, I will not let him go. For she believes that she can retain him, and that he had only distanced himself from her because of some fault she had committed. I will hold him so strongly, she continues to say, and I will attach myself to him with such fidelity, that I will not let him go, until...

<summary>The Soul moves beyond the help of angels to find the Beloved herself, experiencing a moment of spiritual ecstasy and a firm resolve to maintain this divine union.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual marriage, divine union, seeking God, ecstasy, the Soul, the Beloved, fidelity</keywords>

...I have brought him into my mother’s house, into the bosom of God, which is the chamber of her who bore me; since he is my principle and my origin. But what are you saying, innocent Soul? Is it not for him to lead you there, and not for you to lead him? Love believes all things <margin>* John 20:15.</margin> possible, just as it * persuaded Mary Magdalene that she could carry away the body of her Lord <note>original: "Madelaine." The author refers to the scene at the tomb where Mary asks the gardener for the body, not considering its weight or the logistics.</note>. The passion she feels to go there makes her—without considering that she must be there with him, and clothed in him—say that she wishes to introduce him there.

VERSE 5.
I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the hinds of the field, that you stir not up, nor awake my beloved, until she please. <note>original Latin: "Adjuro vos filiæ Ierusalem per capreas cervosque camporũ, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectã, donec ipsa velit."</note>
</margin>

*I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the gazelles and by the stags of the field, not to interrupt the sleep of my Beloved, and not to wake her until she so desires.*

**T**he Bridegroom, full of compassion, after this first trial of his Bride (which is at least the first strong and intimate trial) since she began...

<summary>The author explains the Soul's bold desire to lead the Divine back to its own origin, comparing her holy audacity to Mary Magdalene's love. The text then introduces Verse 5, where the Bridegroom commands that the Soul's spiritual rest not be disturbed.</summary>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>The Song of Songs</header>

...to rise and come out, he shares with her once more his <term>essential union<gloss>union essentielle: A mystical state where the soul is united with God in its deepest essence, rather than just through emotions or intellect.</gloss></term>. Then this poor Soul is so ravished by a blessing that appears infinitely greater to her than before, because it cost her more dearly; she falls asleep, she swoons, she loses herself, and seems to expire in the arms of love. One can see from this that although the Soul suffers much in the search for her Beloved, nevertheless her pains are but shadows of pain when compared to the happiness of possessing this adorable object. This is why <margin>* Romans 8. v. 18. <note>The original text cites verse 8, but the quote corresponds to Romans 8:18.</note></margin> Saint Paul said * that even the greatest pains of this life have no proportion to the glory that shall be revealed in us. Her Beloved does not wish her to be awakened, because this awakening would prevent her death and delay her happiness. <note>The "death" mentioned here is "mystical death," a state where the soul's own ego and self-will are entirely surrendered to God.</note>

*Who is she who comes up from the desert, like a column of smoke perfumed with myrrh and frankincense, and all the powders of the merchant?* <note>Original Latin: "Quæ est ista, quæ ascendit per desertum, sicut virgula fumi ex aromatibus myrrhæ, & thuris, & universi pulveris pigmentarii?"</note>

*Who is she who rises up through the desert, like a small vapor of spices, of myrrh, and of incense, and of all sorts of powders from skilled perfumers.*

The friends of the Bride <note>In this mystical commentary, the "friends" are often understood as the "daughters of Jerusalem" or angelic spirits who observe the Soul's progress.</note>, seeing her adorned with so many perfections and filled with so many graces as a result of the Bridegroom’s visit, express their wonder with these words: *Who is she who rises through the desert like a small vapor of spices?* This is because the Lover <note>The "Amante" or the Soul.</note> is purified so thoroughly within the arms of her Bridegroom that she emerges from them like a subtle vapor, which the fire of love has nearly consumed. She is like a vapor tending upward because of her <term>uprightness<gloss>droiture: In mystical theology, this refers to a soul that is perfectly aligned with God's will, stripped of all selfish "bends" or inclinations.</gloss></term> and her justice; she is a "subtle vapor" to show that she is already entirely spirit. This vapor is composed of the most exquisite scents of all the virtues: but it must be observed that the scents forming this vapor are resins intended to be melted, and powders

### VERSE 8.

<margin>
All hold swords, and are most expert in war: every man’s sword is upon his thigh because of fears in the night.
<note>Original Latin: "Omnes tenétes gladios, & ad bella doctissimi: uniuscujusque ensis super femur suú, propter timores nocturnos."</note>
</margin>

*All are armed with their sword, and are very skillful in war; each of them has his sword at his side, because of the fears of the night.*

<margin>* Psalm 71:16-17 <note>The original cites "Pseaum. 70. v. 17," following the Vulgate numbering.</note></margin>
All are armed with their sword to fight with power against this Soul, who, through a secret presumption, wishes to attribute to herself what belongs only to God. This is what makes them say with one voice: *Who is like God?* <note>This is the literal meaning of the name "Michael."</note> Divine Justice is the first to come and fight, destroying the creature's <term>self-righteousness<gloss>The soul's mistaken belief in its own inherent goodness or merit apart from God.</gloss></term>; then Divine Strength comes to strike down the man's own strength. By making the soul *enter through the experience of her extreme weakness into the power of the Lord, she is taught to remember no other justice but that of God alone.

Divine Providence declares itself against human foresight; and it is the same with all the At-

<summary>The author explains that the "sixty valiant men" are Divine Attributes that use their "swords" to strip the contemplative soul of its pride and self-reliance. This painful process of "nightly fears" is necessary to lead the soul toward a state where it relies solely on God's justice and power.</summary>

attributes. They are all armed, because the soul must be destroyed in all these things to be admitted into the <term>bed of Solomon<gloss>A mystical symbol for the state of perfect union and rest between the soul and God.</gloss></term>, to be wed, and so that the marriage may be completed and consummated. These valiant warriors always have a sword at their side. This sword is none other than the most intimate and penetrating Word of God; but an effective word which, by uncovering the soul's most secret presumption, tears it away from her at the same time.

This word is the <term>uncreated Word<gloss>The eternal Logos or Son of God, who exists before time and through whom all things were created.</gloss></term>, which manifests in the depths of the soul only to perform there what it expresses. No sooner is it declared than, like a thunderbolt, it reduces to powder anything that opposes its passage. This divine Word, in becoming incarnate, acted in the very same way: <margin>* Psalm 33:9 <note>The original cites "Psal. 32. v. 9," following the Vulgate numbering.</note></margin> *He spoke, and it was done*, and He imprinted the characteristics of His Omnipotence upon His Humanity. He came in the lowliness of the creature to destroy its pride, and in its weakness to strike down its strength; and He took the form of the <term>sinner<gloss>The French "pécheur" refers to Christ taking on the likeness of sinful flesh (Romans 8:3) to redeem humanity.</gloss></term> to overthrow self-righteousness. He does the same within the soul: He humbles her, He weakens her, He covers her with mi- <note>The text breaks here; the word is likely "misère" (misery or wretchedness), continued on the following page.</note>

<term>Word</term><gloss>In Christian theology, "the Word" (Greek: *Logos*) refers to the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ, in his eternal, divine nature.</gloss>, is clearly expressed by the support of this mysterious seat, which was all of gold. For often in Scripture, gold is taken to represent God himself. The ascent of this divine throne is adorned with purple, which expresses very well that although the bosom of God the Father—which is the dwelling of the Word—belonged to him by his <term>eternal generation</term><gloss>The theological concept that the Son is eternally "begotten" or brought forth by the Father, sharing the same divine essence.</gloss>; and that he could have no other dwelling; although he had made himself man by the decree of divine Justice, to which he had voluntarily submitted; nevertheless, he could not ascend back to his Father to enter into the fullness of his glory except through the purple of his Blood: *Since it was necessary that the Christ should endure great sufferings, and die, and that thus he should enter into his glory.* <note>original: "Puisqu'il a fallu que le Christ endurât de grands maux, & mourût, & qu'ainsi il entrât dans sa gloire." This is a paraphrase of Luke 24:26.</note> The middle, and all the interior of this place of triumph, is furnished with ornaments of very great price, which are well understood under the name of <term>charity</term><gloss>From the Latin *caritas*, meaning the highest form of Christian love or divine grace, rather than merely giving to the poor.</gloss>, as being that which is greatest and most precious. And is it not in Jesus Christ that *all the treasures* and *the fullness of the Divinity* reside? It is to him that the Holy Spirit has been given beyond all measure. The Holy Spirit, then, fills the middle and all

the interior of this majestic throne; since he is the love of the Father and the Son; and also the love by which God loves humanity: and just as he is the union of the Divine persons, he is also the bond that ties pure souls to Jesus Christ. Now, the divine <term>Solomon</term><gloss>In this mystical tradition, King Solomon is viewed as a "type" or symbolic figure representing Jesus Christ, the true Prince of Peace.</gloss> did all this in consideration of the daughters of Jerusalem, who are his elect for whom he did everything and suffered everything.

In the Sanctuary that God builds for himself in his <term>Beloved</term><gloss>The original French *Amante* is the feminine form of "lover," traditionally referring to the individual Soul or the Church in its relationship with God.</gloss>, there are likewise columns of silver, which are the gifts of the Holy Spirit, established upon divine grace, which is like pure and shining silver that serves as their material and foundation. ### VERSE 5.

<margin>
Your two breasts are like two twin fawns of a gazelle, which feed among the lilies. <note>original Latin: "Duo ubera tua sicut duo hinnuli capreæ gemelli, qui pascunt in liliis."</note>
</margin>
*Your two breasts are like two little twins of the doe, who graze among the lilies.*

The Bride <note>The original French text uses "L'Epouse" (the Bride), representing the Soul in this mystical tradition, though OCR may sometimes misread this as "L'Epoux" (the Groom).</note> here receives the capacity to assist souls, represented by her breasts <note>In mystical theology, the "breasts" of the soul symbolize its ability to nourish others through spiritual instruction and charity, having been "filled" by God.</note>; but she does not receive it in all the fullness that will be communicated to her later: this capacity is merely imprinted upon her, like a seed of fertility, the abundance of which is signified by the little twins of the doe. They are twins because they spring from a single source, which is Jesus Christ; they graze among the lilies, since they nourish themselves on the purity of doctrine and through the examples set by the same Jesus Christ.

<margin>VERSE 6.</margin>
<margin>Until the day breathes, and the shadows decline, I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense. <note>original Latin: "Donec aſpiret dies, & inclinetur umbre, vadā ad montē myrrhæ, & ad collem thuris."</note></margin>
*Until the day appears, and the shadows fall; I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of incense.*

The Groom <note>In this tradition of mystical commentary, the "Groom" (L'Epoux) represents Jesus Christ.</note> interrupts the praise of his Beloved <note>The "Beloved" (Amante) represents the Soul seeking union with God.</note>, to invite her to follow him toward the mountain where the myrrh grows, and even to the hills upon which the incense is gathered. Until, he says, the day of the new life, which you are to receive in my Father, begins to dawn; and until the shadows, which keep you in the darkness of <term>the barest faith</term> <note>A technical term in mystical theology (foi la plus nue) referring to a state where the soul has no sensory or intellectual "proof" of God, but trusts Him in complete obscurity.</note>, decline and dissipate: I will go to the mountain of myrrh; because you will find me no longer except in bitterness and in the cross. This will nonetheless be, for me, a mountain of a most pleasing scent; since the scent of your sufferings will rise toward me, like incense; and it will be through these sufferings that I shall take my rest in you.

### VERSE 7.

<margin>You are all beautiful, my love, and there is no spot in you. <note>original Latin: "Tota pulchra es amica mea , & macula nõ eſt in te."</note></margin>
->*My Beloved, you are all beautiful,*<-
->*& there is no spot in you.*<-

Until the soul had been completely melted in bitterness and on the cross, although she was beautiful, she was nonetheless not *all* beautiful. But since she has been melted under the weight of trials <note>The original French uses "traverses," implying obstacles or setbacks that test one's path.</note> and afflictions, she is all beautiful, and there remains in her no spot nor deformity. She would thereby be disposed to <term>permanent union</term> <note>In mystical theology, "permanent union" refers to the "Spiritual Marriage," where the soul remains in an uninterrupted state of communion with God.</note>, if her nature—still harsh, narrow, restricted, and limited—did not prevent this happiness. This quality is not a "spot" within her, nor anything that offends God; it is merely a defect of her nature, inherited from Adam <note>This refers to the "fallen" human condition, which persists even in a state of grace until it is fully refined by God.</note>, which her Groom <note>The "Groom" (L'Epoux) represents Jesus Christ.</note> will gradually destroy. But for her part, since the cross has entirely disfigured her in the eyes of men, she is all beautiful in the eyes of her Groom; and since she no longer possesses beauty <note>Referring to external, worldly, or self-centered beauty.</note>, she has found the true beauty.

<vocab>Song of Songs, bitterness, cross, permanent union, Adam, Groom, beauty, "You are all beautiful"</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the soul achieves perfect spiritual beauty only after being "melted" through suffering and the cross. It distinguishes between a "spot" of sin and the inherent limitations of human nature, which Christ gradually refines toward total union.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual refinement, suffering, beauty in Christ, human nature, the Cross, mystical union, Adam, the Groom</keywords>

> *Come from Lebanon, my Spouse, come from Lebanon, come, and you shall be crowned; from the heights of Amana, from the summit of Sanir, and of Hermon, from the dens of Lions, and from the mountains of Leopards.*

The Groom <note>Christ, in the context of this mystical commentary.</note> calls her here by the name of Spouse, and invites her to hasten to let herself be consumed, destroyed, and annihilated <note>In the French mystical tradition, "annihilation" refers to the complete surrender of the self-will so that only God acts within the soul.</note>, and to accept the <term>spiritual marriage</term> <note>The "Spiritual Marriage" (unio mystica) is considered the highest state of the interior life, representing a permanent and indissoluble union between the soul and God.</note>. He calls her to be wedded and crowned. But O Groom, shall I say it? why invite a Spouse so strongly, and for so long, to a wedding for which she is so passionately eager? You call her from Lebanon, even though she is in Jerusalem! Is it because you sometimes give the name of Lebanon to Jerusalem, or to mark, by the height of this famous mountain, the elevation where she has already arrived before your eyes? She has almost no more distance to travel to be united to you by an immortal knot; and when she appears to approach your bed, she is...

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs 4:8. The previous page ended mid-word with "im...", likely intending "immédiatement" (immediately) or "impitoyablement" (pitilessly), describing the Soul's approach to union before being seemingly turned away.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
# the Song of Songs

...repelled by sixty strong men <note>A reference to the "sixty valiant men" from Song of Songs 3:7 who guard Solomon's bed, often interpreted in this tradition as the rigors of divine justice or the trials a soul must pass.</note>. Is there not a certain cruelty in drawing her so strongly, though with such sweetness, to possess a good that she prizes more than a thousand lives, and then, when she is near to its possession, to rebuff her so rudely? O God, you invite, you call, you grant the disposition for a state before granting the state itself; just as one gives a taste of an exquisite liquor <note>original: "liqueur exquise"</note> in order to make it more desired. Oh, what do you not make this Soul suffer by delaying what you promise her!

*Come then, he says to her, my Spouse.* For there is now but one step left to take to truly be so. Until now, I have called you *My beautiful one, my Beloved, my dove*; but I have not yet called you by the name of Spouse. Oh, how sweet this name is! But the possession of it will be much sweeter and more charming!

*Come, he says again, from the summit of the highest mountains:* that is to say, from the pure practice of the most eminent virtues, signified by the mountains of Amana, Sanir, and Hermon, which are near Mount Lebanon <note>These are peaks in the Anti-Lebanon mountain range. In mystical theology, mountains often symbolize high states of virtue or prayer.</note>. However elevated all this may seem to you,

<summary>The author explores the spiritual agony of the Soul who is invited toward divine union but experiences a painful delay, transitioning from being called 'Beloved' to the formal title of 'Spouse.'</summary>
<keywords>Spiritual Marriage, Soul, Spouse, divine union, Amana, Sanir, Hermon, Mount Lebanon, virtues, mystical theology</keywords>

and though it is so in fact; it is still necessary to climb higher, and to surpass all things to enter with me into the bosom of my Father, and to rest there without any intermediary <note>original: "sans milieu." In mystical theology, this refers to a state of "unmediated" union where no images, thoughts, or created things stand between the soul and God.</note>, and through the loss of every means <note>original: "moïen." The "means" are the spiritual practices, virtues, or intellectual concepts that initially help a soul but must eventually be transcended for direct union.</note>: immediate and central union only taking place above everything created. But come also from the dens of lions, and from the mountains of leopards: for it will only be through the most cruel persecutions of men and of demons, like so many ferocious beasts, that you will be able to arrive at such a divine state. It is time to raise yourself more than ever above all that, since you are ready to be crowned as my Spouse.

### VERSE 9.

<margin>You have wounded my heart, my sister, my spouse; you have wounded my heart with one of your eyes, and with one hair of your neck. <note>original Latin: "Vulnerasti cor meum soror mea spõsa, vulnerasti cor meũ in uno oculorũ, tuorum, & in uno crine colli tui."</note></margin>

*You have wounded my heart, my sister, my Bride: you have wounded my heart with a glance of your eyes, and by the joined hairs of your neck.*

You are my sister, since we belong to the same Father: You are my Bride; since I have already betrothed you, and it requires but very little more for our marriage to be consummated. My sister, my Bride. Oh, words too sweet for a soul afflicted by the fact that the Beauty she loves, and by whom she is so tenderly loved, does not yet allow itself to be fully possessed! *You have wounded my heart*, he says to her, *you have wounded my heart*. You have dealt Him, O Bride, a double wound: one, by a glance of your eyes; as if he were saying: what has wounded and charmed me in you is that all your misfortunes, all your disgraces, and your most extreme sorrows—none of this has moved you to turn your eye away from me to look upon yourself: you 

<meta>Catchword: [you] have not [n'avez]</meta>

<summary>The Bridegroom (Christ) expresses how the soul's singular focus and devotion have "wounded" His heart with love, addressing the soul as both sister and bride to signify their shared divine origin and impending union.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual marriage, wound of love, divine intimacy, constancy, Song of Songs 4:9, mystical union</keywords>

...note <note>Continuing from the previous page: "The Bridegroom, foreseeing all the conquests that his Bride must make for him, [wishes us to] note..."</note> that the more the Bride advances, the more her breasts become full, the Bridegroom filling them for her more and more, which makes him say: *How beautiful are your breasts! They ravish and charm me. They are more beautiful than wine; for they contain both wine and milk; wine for the strong, and milk for children.* <note>In mystical theology, the "breasts" of the soul represent its capacity to nourish others. "Wine" symbolizes strong, challenging doctrine for the spiritually mature, while "milk" represents simpler truths for beginners.</note> The odors by which you draw souls to me infinitely surpass all perfumes: There will be in you a scent that they will only know when they are well advanced, a scent that will draw them and make them run after you, to come to me, and they will be brought to me by you. This secret odor <note>In this context, "odor" or "scent" refers to the spiritual influence or "reputation of sanctity" that a holy person radiates, which attracts others to the divine life without the need for words.</note> will astonish those who do not know this mystery. However, their experience will force them to say: I do not know what it is in you that attracts me: it is an admirable scent, against which one can hardly defend oneself, even though one does not perceive what it is: it must be the anointing of the Spirit, which only the Christ of the Lord can communicate to his Brides.

*Your lips, my Bride, are like the honeycomb from which honey flows: Honey and milk are found under your tongue; and the scent of your garments is like the scent of incense.*

As soon as the soul has arrived at the happiness of being received forever into its God, it becomes both mother and nurse: Fecundity <note>In this mystical context, "fecundity" refers to the soul’s new ability to "give birth" to spiritual life in others.</note> is given to it; it is placed by its state into the Apostolic life, and from then on, the lips of this person are like a honeycomb, which continually distills for the benefit of other souls. It is only her lips, and not her words; because it is the Bridegroom who speaks through his Bride, and the lips of his Bride serve him as an organ to express his divine word. Honey and milk, he says to her, are under the tongue that I give you: it is I who put this honey and this milk under your tongue, and who causes them to be spread by you for the benefit of souls, according—
