her actions, none of which are not entirely for me: she claims no ownership <note>The French *proprietaire* refers to the spiritual concept of "propriété," or self-appropriation, where a soul mistakenly believes its virtues or actions belong to itself rather than to God.</note> over any action, nor over anything at all. She is closed off on every side; there is nothing in her for herself, nor for any other creature. She is also a fountain, for she is intimately united to me, who am the Source. She must spread these waters throughout the whole earth, yet I keep her sealed, so that not a single drop shall ever flow out except by my command. Thus, the waters she distributes will be perfectly pure and untainted, as they spring directly from my own source.

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>of Solomon.</header>

[ren]dering useful to everyone, the spirit of God will communicate itself through you in various places, just as one sees the pomegranate, which represents souls united in charity, communicating its sap to all the seeds it contains. It is true that the primary meaning of this passage concerns the Church, but one cannot believe the great fruits a truly annihilated soul <term>annihilated soul: In 17th-century French mysticism, this refers to a soul that has completely surrendered its own will and ego (self-annihilation) to become a pure vessel for God's action.</term> would produce for the benefit of mankind once it were applied to helping them.

In this garden, there are fruits of every kind; each soul, while possessing qualities common to the others, also has its own particular character. One excels in charity, and that is the pomegranate; another distinguishes itself by gentleness, and that is the apple; another is distinguished by suffering and by the scent of its good example, and that is the cypress; yet another distills devotion, inner recollection <note>The French *recueïllement* refers to the practice of withdrawing one’s senses and thoughts from the world to focus entirely on the presence of God within.</note>, and peace, and that is the nard; and all are helped by the annihilated Bride according to their needs.

merits; and whose fragrance rose up to God his Father. *I have,* he adds, *eaten my honeycomb.* <note>original: "mangé mon raïon de miel." This refers to Song of Songs 5:1.</note> This is understood as his actions and his doctrine: for he practiced what he preached; and he commanded nothing of us that he did not first put into execution himself, earning for us—through the very things he practiced—the grace to do what he requires of us. Thus, the life of JESUS CHRIST was like a honeycomb, in which Divine order, as well as sweetness, constituted his nourishment and his happiness, in view of the glory his Father received from it and the benefit it brought to humanity. *I have drunk my wine and my milk.* What is this wine that you have drunk, O Divine Savior, and with which you were so deeply intoxicated that you forgot yourself? This wine was the excessive love he bore for humanity, which made him forget he was God <note>In mystical theology, "forgetting he was God" refers to the *kenosis* or "self-emptying," where Christ set aside his divine glory to take on human suffering.</note> so that he might think only of their <term>salvation</term> <note>Salvation (salut) here refers to the deliverance of the soul from sin and its reconciliation with God.</note>. He was so intoxicated by it that it is said of him by a Prophet that he will be satisfied <note>Likely a reference to Isaiah 53:11: "He shall see of the travail of his soul, and shall be satisfied."</note>

The Soul that watches for its God experiences that, although its exterior appears dead, and as if motionless and extinguished—much like a sleeping body—nevertheless, this heart always possesses a secret and unknown vigor <note>original: "vigueur secrette." In mystical theology, this refers to the "interior life" where the spirit remains active even when the physical senses are at rest.</note>, which keeps it united to God. Furthermore, souls far advanced <note>Meaning those who have progressed deep into the stages of prayer and contemplation.</note> often experience a surprising thing: that they have only a "half-sleep" at night, and God works more, it seems, within them during the night and in sleep than during the day. **VERSE 3.**
<note>original Latin: "Expoliavi me tunica mea, quomodo induar illa? Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo illos?"</note>
I have stripped off my robe, how shall I put it on again? I have washed my feet, how shall I soil them?
</margin>
I have stripped myself, how shall I clothe myself again? I have washed my feet, how shall I soil them?

The Bride, seeing that the Bridegroom speaks of making her a partaker of His ignominies <note>In this context, "ignominies" refers to public disgrace, shame, or the loss of one's reputation in the eyes of the world.</note>, is greatly afraid; and as much as she was courageous and intrepid in accepting the cross, she is just as fearful of the abjection <note>The state of being cast down to a low or humble condition; total self-abasement.</note> with which she is threatened. Many are quite willing to carry the cross, but there is almost no one who wishes to carry the infamy of the cross. When ignominy is proposed to this Soul, she apprehends two things: The first is being clothed again in that of which she had been stripped—namely, herself and her natural defects; the other is being soiled by the affections of creatures <note>In mystical writing, "creatures" refers to any created beings or worldly things (people, honors, comforts) as opposed to the Creator.</note>. I have, she says, stripped myself of myself, of my defects, and of what there was in me of the sinful Adam <note>A reference to the "Old Adam," or the fallen human nature inherited from the first man according to Christian doctrine.</note>: how could I ever clothe myself in them again? <margin>VERSE 4.</margin>
<margin><note>original: "Dilectus meus misit manum suã per foramē, & venter meus intremuit ad tactum ejus."</note> My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my heart trembled at his touch.</margin>
*My Beloved reached out his hand through a hole in the door, and my inmost being was stirred at this single touch.*

THE Beloved, despite the resistance of his Bride, reaches his hand through a small passage that remains open to him; this represents a lingering scrap of surrender, maintained despite the aversion the Soul feels toward abandoning herself with such intensity. A Soul at this stage of spiritual life possesses a fundamental submission to all of God’s wishes, such that she would want to refuse Him nothing. However, when God reveals His specific plans for her—using the rights He has acquired over her to demand the final renunciations and the most extreme sacrifices—ah, it is then that her inmost being <note>The original French uses "entrailles," literally "entrails" or "bowels." In 17th-century spiritual writing, this refers to the deepest, most visceral seat of human emotion and the soul's will.</note> is stirred. She finds great difficulty in a place where she thought she no longer had any; and this difficulty arises because she was still attached to some thing—

### VERSE 6.

<margin>*I opened the latch of my door to my beloved; but he had turned aside, and had passed by. *I unbolted my door to open to my Beloved; but he had already turned away and passed by; my soul melted as soon as he spoke: I sought him and I did not find him; I called him and he did not answer me.*

IT IS as if this Soul said: I have lifted the barrier that prevented both my <term>total loss: in mystical theology, the "annihilation" or complete death of the ego/self-will to make room for God</term> and the consummation of my marriage; for this divine marriage cannot be consummated until this total loss has occurred. I have therefore removed this barrier through the most courageous surrender and the purest sacrifice that ever was. I opened to my Beloved, believing that he would enter and that he would heal the pain he had caused me by his touch; but alas, the blow would be too gentle if he brought the remedy so quickly. ...leaves this afflicted Lover with nothing but the wound he has dealt her, the pain of her fault, and the impurity she believes she has contracted by rising. <note>In this mystical context, the "impurity" refers to the Soul's feeling of unworthiness or spiritual "soiling" caused by her initial hesitation to respond to the Divine call.</note> However, the goodness of the Spouse is so great that even though he hides himself, he does not cease to bestow great graces upon his friends; and these graces are all the greater when the deprivations are longer and harsher. This is what he did for his Spouse, who found herself in a new state of being that was very advantageous to her, though she did not yet recognize it as such. It was that her Soul melted and <term>liquefied: a mystical state where the soul’s rigid self-will softens so it can be poured into God</term> as soon as her Beloved had spoken. Through this liquefaction, she lost those hard and narrow qualities that were preventing the consummation of the <term>spiritual marriage: the ultimate union of the soul with the Divine</term>. In this way, she was made entirely ready to flow back into her origin. <note>The "origin" refers to God, the source from which the soul proceeded and to which it seeks to return.</note> *I sought my Beloved, and I did not find him: I called him; but he no longer has a word for me: oh, inconceivable affliction!*

Even in his absence, the Spouse is seen as working a greater grace within the Lover by removing her internal obstacles.</summary>

### VERSE 6.

<margin>
I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved; but he had turned aside, and had passed by. My soul melted when he spoke: I sought him, and I did not find him: I called him, and he did not answer me. <note>original Latin: "Pessulum ostii mei aperui dilecto meo ; at ille declinaverat , atque transierat. > *I unbolted my door to open to my Beloved: but he had already turned away, and he had passed on; my soul melted as soon as he spoke: I sought him and I found him not, I called him and he did not answer me.*

It is as if this Soul said: I have lifted the barrier that prevented both my <term>total loss: the mystical process of "annihilation" or the setting aside of the individual ego to make room for God</term> and the consummation of my marriage; for this <term>divine marriage: the highest state of spiritual union between the soul and the Divine</term> cannot be consummated until this total loss has occurred. I have therefore removed this barrier through the most courageous abandonment and the purest sacrifice that ever was. I opened to my Beloved, believing that he would enter and would heal the pain he had caused me by his touch; but alas, the blow would be too sweet if he brought the remedy so promptly. ### VERSE 7:

<margin>The watchmen who go around the city found me; they struck me and wounded me; the keepers of the walls took away my cloak from me. <note>original Latin: "Invenerunt me custodes qui circumeunt civitatem: percusserunt me, & vulneraverunt me: tulerunt pallium meũ mihi custodes murorum."</note></margin>
*The watchmen who go the rounds of the city met me; they beat me and wounded me; the keepers of the walls took away my cloak.*

Unfortunate Spouse! <note>In this mystical tradition, the "Spouse" represents the individual human soul seeking union with God.</note> Nothing like this had ever happened to you before, because until now your Groom was guarding you; you rested safely under his shadow: you were secure within his arms; but since he has distanced himself because of your fault, ah, what has become of you! You thought you had suffered much through the many trials he had already made of your fidelity; yet those were small things compared to what remains for you to suffer. What you suffered while with him was but the shadow of suffering, and you should have expected nothing less: do you believe you can marry a God torn by wounds, pierced by nails, and stripped of everything, without being treated the same? This <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing the thought that the soul must be "stripped" of its own defenses—the "cloak"—to achieve perfect union.</note>

<vocab>Song of Songs, Spouse, trials, fidelity, wounds, nails, treated</vocab>

<summary>This page comments on Verse 7, explaining that the Soul's previous sufferings were minor compared to the "stripping" she must now endure. The author argues that to be united with a crucified God, the Soul must also undergo a process of being wounded and divested of her protections.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Solomon, mystical suffering, the Spouse, spiritual trials, imitation of Christ, the watchmen, purgation</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Chapter 5, Verse 7 of the Song of Songs. Having established that the Soul (the Spouse) must follow the path of the suffering Groom, the text now describes the specific "stripping" she undergoes at the hands of the city guards.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>->of Solomon.<-</header>

The Soul finds herself beaten and wounded by all those who guard the city; those who until now had not dared to attack her, and who nevertheless watched her incessantly, now take their time to strike her. Who are these guards? They are the ministers of the Justice of God: They wound her, and they take away from her the cloak of her own righteousness <note>In mystical theology, "own righteousness" (propre justice) refers to a soul's pride in its own good works or spiritual progress, which must be stripped away to rely solely on God's grace.</note>, which was so dear to her. O unfortunate Spouse, what will you do in such a pitiful state? The Groom will no longer want you after such a sad accident, which brings with it the debasement of having been mistreated by soldiers, and being covered in wounds, even to the point of having left your cloak in their hands, though it was your principal ornament. If you still continue to seek your Beloved, people will say that you are foolish to present yourself to him in this manner: and furthermore, if you stop seeking him, you will die of languor <note>original: "langueur"; here meaning a spiritual wasting away or an intense, painful longing.</note>; your state is certainly deplorable.

### VERSE 8.

<margin>I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you tell him that I languish with love. <note>original Latin: "Adjuro vos filiæ Jeruſalem, ſi inveneritis dilectum meum, ut nuncietis ei quia amore langueo."</note></margin>

> I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you should meet my Beloved, to tell him that I languish with love.

True love has no eyes to look upon itself. This afflicted Lover forgets her own wounds, even though they are still bleeding; she no longer remembers her loss, nor does she even speak of it. She thinks only of the one she loves, and she seeks him with all the more strength as she encounters more obstacles to possessing him. She addresses the **Interior Souls** <note>The "Interior Souls" (Ames interieures) refers to those who have advanced in the practice of internal prayer and contemplative life, and are thus more likely to "find" the Divine Beloved.</note> and says to them: "O you to whom my Beloved will undoubtedly reveal himself, I adjure you by him to tell him that I languish with love for him." What? O most beautiful of women, do you not wish for them to speak to him instead of your wounds, and to tell him what you have suffered while seeking him? "No, no," answers this

<vocab>Song of Songs, Beloved, Jerusalem, love, Interior Souls, I languish</vocab>

<summary>In this passage, the Soul demonstrates the selflessness of divine love by ignoring her own recent injuries to focus entirely on her longing for the Beloved. She begs other spiritually advanced souls to relay her state of "love-sickness" to him, prioritizing her devotion over her suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Solomon, divine love, spiritual longing, Interior Souls, self-forgetfulness, mysticism</keywords>

Generous soul, I am too well rewarded for my sufferings, since I have endured them for him, and I prefer them to the greatest riches. Tell only one thing to my Beloved: it is that I languish with love for him. The wound that his love has made in the depths of my heart is so vivid that I am insensitive to all external pains; I even dare to say that, compared to that internal wound, the external ones feel like refreshments to me.

**VERSE 13.**
His cheeks are like beds of spices, prepared by the perfumers. His lips are lilies dripping with the finest myrrh. <note>original Latin: "Genæ illius sicut areolæ aromatum, confitæ à pigmentariis. Labia illius lilia distillantia myrrham primam."</note>

And just as the cheeks are joined to the head, so this noble and beautiful Soul is joined to the Divinity. It is certainly a skilled perfumer who made this choice, and who arranged them so well; since it is the Holy Spirit who has given such a beautiful order to the entire interior and exterior of JESUS CHRIST. ### VERSE 15.

<margin>His legs are like pillars of marble, founded upon bases of gold. His appearance is like Lebanon, chosen like the cedars. <note>original Latin: "Crura illius columnæ marmoreæ, quæ fundatæ sunt super bases aureas. Species ejus ut libani, electus ut cedri."</note></margin>
*His legs are like pillars of marble set upon bases of gold. His beauty equals that of Lebanon; he is chosen like the cedars.*

The entire lower part of the body, which is spoken of here under the name of the legs and the feet that support them, is taken specifically to represent the flesh of the Savior; and it is aptly designated by marble because of its incorruptibility. ### VERSE 17. <note>In modern Bibles, this verse is categorized as Chapter 6, Verse 1.</note>

<margin>Where has your beloved gone, O most beautiful among women? Where has your beloved turned aside? And we will seek him with you? <note>original Latin: "Quò abiit dilectus tuus, ô pulcherrima mulierum? qua declinavit dilectus tuus? & quaeremus eum tecum?"</note></margin>

O victorious love; when you flee most strongly, it is then that you make the most conquests! And this Soul, which is like an impetuous torrent because of her violent love, carries away with her everything she encounters. O, who would not desire to see and seek such a desirable Lover? > *Turn your eyes away from me; for they have made me fly away. Your hair is like a flock of goats that appeared from Gilead.*

ONE cannot believe the delicacy of God's love, & the extreme purity he demands of the Souls who are his Spouses <note>In mystical theology, the "Spouse" or "Bride" represents the human soul, while the "Groom" represents Christ.</note>; what makes the perfection of one state is the imperfection of another: formerly the Groom praised himself infinitely that his Bride never turned her gaze from him: & today he does not want her to look at him, he says that the Bride's gaze makes him fly away. It must even then lose the perceived sight of God, & all distinct knowledge <term>distinct knowledge|A theological term for spiritual insights that the mind can clearly define or "feel." ### VERSE 6.

<margin>As the rind of a pomegranate, so are your cheeks, besides your hidden parts. <note>original Latin: "Sicut cortex mali punici, sic genæ tuæ, absque occultis tuis."</note></margin>

> *Your cheeks resemble the rind of a pomegranate, without what is hidden within you.*

JUST as the rind is the least part of the pomegranate, which contains within itself all its goodness: so also that which appears externally of the <term>Soul: "Ame," the spiritual essence of the person</term> at this degree, is a very small thing compared to what is hidden. The interior is full of the purest charity and the most <term>reserved graces: "graces reservées," spiritual gifts or favors kept secret between God and the soul</term>, covered nevertheless by a very common exterior; for God takes pleasure in hiding the Souls He wants for Himself: men are not worthy of knowing them, and the Angels admire and respect them, though under the simplest exterior in the world. The jealousy of God is as harsh as hell, in that there is nothing He will not do to fully possess His Brides.

**V**ERSE 7.
There are sixty queens, and eighty concubines, and of young maidens there is no number. <note>original Latin: "Sexaginta sunt reginæ, & octoginta concubinæ, & adolescentularum non est numerus."</note>

*There are sixty Queens; and eighty other women, and the young girls are without number.*

The <term>Bridegroom: Christ</term> says that there are <term>Souls: "Ames," the internal spiritual life of the person</term> chosen from among all others, like Queens; others who participate in his singular favors, even though they do not have the status of <term>Sovereigns: "Souveraines," those who have reached the highest state of spiritual authority or perfection</term>; and a great number of young hearts, who belong to him in a common manner, and who begin to sigh for union with him. But this <term>Beloved: the soul in a state of high devotion</term> surpasses them all in the affection he has for her. O God, to what happiness have you raised your <term>Bride: the soul in perfect union with God</term>! There are some who appear like Queens raised above others by the brilliance of their <term>virtues: the moral and spiritual qualities of the soul</term>; there are several others with whom you share your caresses <note>In mystical writing, "caresses" refer to spiritual consolations or felt experiences of God's presence.</note>; but this one Bride is more to you than all the others together.

<summary>The author interprets the biblical "queens, concubines, and maidens" as a hierarchy of spiritual maturity, explaining that while many souls receive divine favor, the "Bride" represents a unique and supreme level of intimacy with God.</summary>

# of Solomon. VERSE 8.

My dove is unique; she is my all-perfect one; she is the only one of her mother, and her mother takes pleasure only in her. The daughters saw her, and they declared her most happy; the Queens and the other women also praised her.

Although the primary meaning of this verse is in favor of the divine Mary and the <term>universal Church: the global community of believers</term>, nevertheless, as nothing is attributed to the Church as a <term>mystical body: the spiritual union of all Christians as one entity</term> that is not also attributed in proportion to <term>souls: the internal spiritual life of the person</term> as its members—especially when they are perfectly pure—so one can say that there are souls whom God has chosen in every age in a most singular way. God, therefore, says that this Soul in whom the marriage has been perfectly consummated, through her total <term>annihilation: "anéantissement," the mystical process of losing one's own will and ego to be entirely filled with God</term> and by her entire loss of self, is a dove in simpli-

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Bride from the Song of Songs, transitioning from the soul’s private union with God to how this state is perceived by others. It builds on the previous page's theme of the soul being "unique" and "perfect" by being lost in the divine.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>of Solomon.</header>

It is in her that Wisdom—which engendered and produced her only to lose her within its own bosom—takes its sole pleasure. The most interior souls <note>Highly advanced spiritual seekers who focus on the inner life rather than outward ritual.</note> have seen her: for God usually allows such persons to be known a little, sometimes giving a measure of discernment regarding their state to other highly spiritual souls. These souls are ravished by this knowledge, and admiring their perfection, they declare them blessed. The "Queens"—who are those elevated souls esteemed by everyone—and also the common souls inferior in merit, likewise give them great praise, because they feel the effect of the grace that is communicated to them.

Although this seems to contradict what was said earlier <note>Referring to the soul being hidden or "annihilated" from the world's view.</note>, there is nonetheless no contradiction: for what is said here relates to the <term>apostolic state: a stage in mystical development where the soul, after reaching union with God, returns to outward activity to serve others, mirroring the life of the Apostles</term> of JESUS CHRIST. We know He was received at one time as King and Savior in the same place where, shortly after, He was put to death like a criminal.

<vocab>Solomon, wisdom, interior souls, knowledge, perfection, Queens, grace, apostolic state, JESUS CHRIST, Savior</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the perfected soul, though lost in God, is recognized and praised by other spiritual "Queens" and common souls. It reconciles this public recognition with the soul's hidden nature by comparing it to the "apostolic state" of Christ, who was both glorified and persecuted.</summary>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>The Song of Songs</header>

...cited, and that she is unique, in that there are few who resemble her; she is also unique because she is reduced back into God, into the perfect unity of her origin. She is most perfect, but through the perfections of God Himself, and because she is exempt from all self-ownership <note>original: "propriété". In mystical theology, this refers to the ego's tendency to claim its actions or virtues as its own rather than attributing them to God.</note>, and released from her hard, narrow, and limited nature, as soon as, through her entire flowing back <note>original: "recoulement". This term describes the soul "flowing back" into God like a stream returning to the ocean.</note>, she has entered into the innocence of God. She is perfect in her core through the loss of all self-seeking. It must be noted that whatever praises the Bridegroom <note>The male figure in the Song of Songs, interpreted here as Christ or God.</note> had given until now to his Bride <note>The female figure, interpreted as the individual soul.</note>, he had not yet said (until she was entirely flowed back into his divine unity) that she was unique and perfect; this is because these qualities are found only in God, when one is entirely conformed to Him through a permanent and lasting state.

She is the "only one of her mother," in that having lost all the multiplicity of her nature, she finds herself alone and separated from all that is natural.

### The Song of Songs

It is in her that Wisdom—who begot and brought her forth only to lose her within her own bosom <note>In mystical theology, "losing" the soul in God's bosom refers to the state of "divine union" where the individual's self-will is absorbed into the Divine.</note>—takes its sole delight. The most interior souls have seen her; for God usually allows such persons to be known a little, sometimes giving a measure of discernment regarding their state to other highly spiritual souls, who are delighted by this knowledge, and who, admiring their perfection, declare them blessed. The "Queens," who are those elevated souls esteemed by all the world, and also the common souls inferior in merit, likewise grant them great praise, because they feel the effect of the grace that is communicated to them.

Although this seems to contradict what was said earlier, there is nevertheless no contradiction; for what is said here refers to the Apostolic State <term>Apostolic State: A stage of mystical life where the soul, after reaching union with God, is called to an outward life of service and ministry, mirroring the public life of Jesus.</term> of JESUS CHRIST, who is known to have been received at one time as King and Savior in the same place where, shortly thereafter, he was put to death as a criminal.

VERSE 9.

Who is she who comes forth like the rising dawn, fair as the moon, bright as the sun, terrible as an army set in battle array? <note>original Latin: "Quæ est ista, quæ progreditur quasi aurora consurgens, pulchra ut luna, electa ut sol, terribilis ut castrorú acies ordinata?"</note>

*Who is she who advances like the rising dawn, who is beautiful as the moon, pure and radiant as the sun, terrible as an army arrayed for battle.*

These are the choirs of the Bridegroom’s <note>In this mystical commentary, the "Bridegroom" represents Jesus Christ.</note> companions, who admire the beauty of his Bride <note>The "Bride" represents the individual human soul in a state of union with the Divine.</note>. *Who is she*, they say, *who advances*, rising little by little; for it must be known that the Soul, although having arrived in God, rises little by little and is perfected in this divine life until she reaches the eternal dwelling. She rises in God imperceptibly, like the dawn, until she reaches her perfect day and her completed noon, which is the glory of Heaven. But this eternal day begins in this life. *She is beautiful as the moon*, because she draws all her beauty from her sun <note>This imagery suggests that the soul has no light of its own, but reflects the light of God, just as the moon reflects the sun.</note>; *She is pure and radiant as the sun*, because she is united to JESUS CHRIST, to be part—

**VERSE 11.**

I knew not:
my soul
troubled

the chariots of

<note>original Latin: "Nescivi : anima mea conturbavit me propter quadrigas Aminadab."</note>

*I knew nothing of it: my soul was troubled within me because of the chariots of Aminadab.*

I did this, she says, without thinking about it and without believing I was doing wrong, or that I was displeasing my Bridegroom <note>Representing Christ.</note>. However, I had no sooner committed this fault than my soul was troubled by the chariots of Aminadab: that is to say, by a thousand upon thousand <term>reflections</term> <note>The author uses "reflections" to describe the mind's tendency to obsessively analyze its own state, which was seen as a distraction from the peace of divine union.</note> that rolled through my head like so many wretched chariots that would have led to my ruin, if the hand of my Beloved had not sustained me.

### The Song of Solomon

### On Solomon

<margin>
VERSE 2.

Your navel is a bowl; a rounded one never lacking in liquor. Your belly is like a heap of wheat, fenced with lilies. <note>original Latin: "Umbilicus tuus crater; tornatilis numquam indigens poculis. Venter tuus sicut acervus tritici, vallatus liliis."</note>
</margin>

*Your navel is like a great cup, fashioned all around, which is never empty of liquor. Your belly is like a heap of wheat surrounded by lilies.*

---

By the navel is meant the soul’s capacity to receive, or its passive disposition <note>In mystical theology, a "passive disposition" refers to a state where the soul stops its own restless activity to let God act within it.</note>, which is extended and enlarged to infinity, since it has been withdrawn into God. This is not only so that the soul may receive divine communications itself, but also to conceive and give birth to many souls for Jesus Christ. It is described as "round" because it receives much, and because it can retain nothing—receiving only in order to pour out. The soul is, at the same time, both fit to receive and ready to distribute, sharing in this the qualities of its Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ.</note>. It is always watered by the spring waters that flow from the divinity, and the most reserved graces are given to it, so as to dis-

<sig>L iij</sig>

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of the bride's anatomy in the Song of Solomon, viewing the "navel" as the soul's infinite capacity to receive and distribute divine grace. It emphasizes that the soul's spiritual "roundness" represents a selfless cycle of receiving from God to give to others.</summary>

### The Song of Songs

tribute to others. Your belly, that is to say, your spiritual fruitfulness, is like a heap of wheat, so abundant is it; it sprouts, grows, bears fruit, and nourishes like wheat, and it possesses all its qualities. But it is surrounded by lilies, as a sign of an entire purity.

VERSE 3.
Your two breasts are like two twin fawns of a roe deer. <note>original Latin: "Duo ubera tua, sicut duo hinnuli gemelli capreæ."</note>
</margin>

->*Your two breasts are like two little twins of the roe deer.*<-

It would be a small thing for this Bride to give birth to souls for her Spouse <note>The Bride represents the perfected soul or the Church; the Spouse represents Christ.</note>, if she were not given the means to nourish them. Thus, the Spouse speaks here of her breasts, to signify that she is not only a mother but also a nurse. In effect, she has that which is necessary to give to her children with such abundance that her breasts are always full, even though she empties them incessantly, and there is not a moment when she does not open them in favor of someone; and though—
